Borderline gradient regularity estimates for quasilinear parabolic systems with data independent of time
Karthik Adimurthi, Wontae Kim

Abstract
In this paper, we study some regularity issues concerning the gradient of weak solutions of u t − div ⁡ 𝒜 ⁡ ( x , t , ∇ u ) = g , u_{t}-\dv\mathcal{A}(x,t,\nabla u)=g, where 𝒜 ⁡ ( x , t , ∇ u ) \displaystyle\mathcal{A}(x,t,\nabla u) is modeled after the p \displaystyle p -Laplace operator. The main results we are interested in is to obtain optimal conditions on the datum g \displaystyle g (independent of time) such that borderline higher integrability of the gradient and Lipschitz estimates for the weak solution holds. Moreover, we develop a theory where we can obtain elliptic type estimates using parabolic theory, which gives improved potential estimates for the elliptic systems.



2020 MSC
: 35D30, 35K55, 35K65.

1
Introduction

In this paper, we are interested in studying the gradient regularity of weak solutions of the system

u_{t}-\dv(|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u)=g(x,t),

(1.1)

where the datum
\displaystyle|g(x,t)|\leq f(x)
is independent of time. Our main goal is to obtain elliptic type estimates using parabolic techniques and as a goal, obtain essentially optimal endpoint conditions required for such regularity.

1.1
Discussion about Gradient higher integrability

The first part of the paper deals with the higher integrability of weak solutions for systems with data independent of time. In the case

u_{t}-\dv|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u=\dv|F(x,t)|^{p-2}F(x,t),

we have the following implications: there exists
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}
such that for any
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, if
\displaystyle F\in L^{p+\varepsilon}_{\loc}
, then we have
\displaystyle|\nabla u|\in L^{p+\varepsilon}
. The natural question to ask is when
\displaystyle\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, how much higher integrability for the gradient of the solution can be retained? Such an end point result was obtained for elliptic systems in
[
3
]
.

The first result we have from
Theorem

2.2
states that if the data
\displaystyle g=f(x)\in L^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}\log L
, then
\displaystyle|\nabla u|\in L^{p}\log L
, so in particular, we obtain a logarithmic improvement of integrability.

Our approach is primarily inspired by the ideas developed in
[
13
]
where they developed the intrinsic scaling techniques required to study higher integrability of the gradient of the solutions of
Eq.

1.1
. In our situation, we cannot directly apply the techniques from
[
13
]
for two reasons, the first being that the datum is a function whose scaling relations are not entirely obvious and the second being, since the data is independent of time, we need to somehow obtain estimates for the parabolic system on every time slice as opposed to over space-time cylinders.

We overcome the first obstacle by considering the intrinsic scaling of the form
Eq.

5.3
and
Eq.

5.4
which captures the right relation between the datum
\displaystyle f(x)
and
\displaystyle|\nabla u|
. To overcome the second difficulty, we make use of an idea from
[
23
]
, where we use test functions depending on time as in
Lemma

5.4
, which is possible thanks to the cancellation from
[
21
, Lemma 5.4]
. An important advantage we gain from such a choice of test function is that we do not need any ’gluing type’ lemma (see
[
13
, Lemma 3.1]
for an example of such a lemma) and as a consequence, our proof simplifies the calculations even when
\displaystyle g\equiv 0
.

In the case
\displaystyle g=g(x,t)
, we can write the datum
\displaystyle g
in divergence form for almost every time slice using the Riesz potential of order one as in
Lemma

3.7
. In this form, we can now directly apply the result from
[
13
]
to obtain
Theorem

2.4
. Applying the Riesz potential bounds, we obtain
Corollary

2.5
which proves higher integrability of the gradient of the solution with an elliptic type condition for the datum in the space variable and the scaling deficit is required only in the time variable.

1.2
Gradient potential estimates

In this section, our main interest is to obtain optimal potential bounds for the gradient of solutions to general nonlinear parabolic systems of the form
Eq.

8.1
which in particular implies the corresponding result for the elliptic systems. We will recall the history of the problem for the elliptic system and refer to
[
16
,
17
,
5
]
for more details regarding this problem. We also highlight the paper
[
4
]
where boundary potential estimates were obtained for more general elliptic systems with
\displaystyle(p,q)
-growth.

The potential estimates for the gradient of the solution were obtained in the important paper
[
20
]
. In particular, they showed that the gradient of solutions can be estimated by linear potentials, noting that these are sharper than Wolff potential bounds when
\displaystyle p\geq 2
(see also
[
10
,
17
,
15
,
7
,
6
,
2
]
for more in this direction).

Our main interest is to obtain improved potential estimates for elliptic systems in the case
\displaystyle p\geq n
and in order to do this, we follow the parabolic approach developed in
[
20
]
. As a consequence, our proof is considerably simpler and requires only
Lemmas

8.10
and
8.12
, which is the main contribution of this section. In
[
5
]
, they were able to obtain boundedness of the gradient of the solution of elliptic systems for the case
\displaystyle p=n=2
under the assumption
\displaystyle f\in L^{q}
with
\displaystyle q\geq\tfrac{3}{2}
, whereas we obtain logarithmic potential which is finite whenever
\displaystyle f\in L^{1+\varepsilon}
for any
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
. We refer to the introduction of
[
5
]
for an overview of the historical development.

In order to highlight the main idea of the proof, we only consider equations of the form
Eq.

8.1
, even though we can consider more general structures as in
[
5
, (1.3)-(1.5)]
and we leave these details to the interested reader. An important aspect of our proof is that we use parabolic techniques developed in
[
16
]
to obtain the required result (see
Theorem

2.7
), which is considerably simpler than those developed in
[
5
]
. In this regard, we also mention the recent papers
[
7
,
6
]
where some interesting potential estimates are obtained for nonautonomous functionals.

2
Main Theorems

Before we state the main theorems, let us recall the scaling deficit exponent given by

d:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{p}{2}&\text{if}\ p\geq 2,\\
\frac{2p}{p(n+2)-2n}&\text{if}\ p\leq 2,\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.1)

Remark 2.1
.

We will use the function
\displaystyle g=g(x,t)
and
\displaystyle f=f(x)
to denote functions that depend on time and are independent of time respectively.

The first theorem we prove is a borderline higher integrability result in the case of the data independent of time.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
\displaystyle u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega))
be a weak solution of
Eq.

1.1
under the assumption
Eq.

3.1
and
\displaystyle|g(x,t)|\leq f(x)
. Furthermore, let
\displaystyle\delta\in(0,1)
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{\bf a}
be as defined in
Definition

5.2
and
Eq.

5.1
respectively. Then for any
\displaystyle a>0
, the following borderline higher integrability result holds:

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{r}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p}\log(e+|\nabla u|^{a})\ dz\apprle_{(n,p,\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1},\delta,a)}\log(e+\lambda_{0}^{a})\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2r}(z_{0})}(|\nabla u|+1)^{p}\ dz+\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|\tilde{f}|\log(e+|\tilde{f}|)\ dz.

Here, we have set

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lambda_{0}^{\frac{p}{d}}:=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2r}(z_{0})}\left(|\nabla u|+1\right)^{p}\ dz+\left(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}(2r)^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}|f|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}\ dx\right)^{\alpha}\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\tilde{f}(x)|:=\lambda_{0}^{\nu}(2r)^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}|f(x)|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}},

where
\displaystyle d
is defined in
Eq.

2.1
and
\displaystyle\alpha,\nu
are as defined in
Definition

5.13
.

Corollary 2.3
.

Following
Theorem

2.2
, given
\displaystyle m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\displaystyle a>0
, let us denote

\log_{(m_{0})}(t):=\underbrace{\log(e+t)\log(e+t)\cdots\log(e+t)}_{m_{0}\text{ times}},

then we have the following borderline higher integrability estimates

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{r}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p}\log_{(m_{0})}(e+|\nabla u|^{a})\ dz&\apprle&\sum_{i=0}^{m_{0}}\log_{(i)}(e+\lambda_{0}^{a})\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2r}(z_{0})}(|\nabla u|+1)^{p}\ dz\\
&&+\sum_{i=0}^{m_{0}}\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|\tilde{f}|\log_{(i)}(e+|\tilde{f}|)\ dz,.\end{array}

From
Lemma

3.8
, we see that a measurable function admits a divergence form representation and thus, we can directly use the higher integrability result from
[
13
]
to conclude the following theorem:

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
\displaystyle u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega))
be a weak solution of
Eq.

1.1
under the assumption
Eq.

3.1
. Then there exists
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(n,p,\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
such that for any
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, there holds

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{r}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p+\varepsilon}\ dz\apprle_{(n,p,\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})}\lambda_{0}^{\varepsilon}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2r}(z_{0})}(|\nabla u|+1)^{p}\ dz+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2r}(z_{0})}|I_{1}g|^{\frac{p}{p-1}+\frac{\varepsilon}{p-1}}\ dz.

Here,
\displaystyle I_{1}
is a Reisz potential of order one (see
Definition

3.6
) and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lambda_{0}^{\frac{p}{d}}:=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2r}(z_{0})}(|\nabla u|+1)^{p}+|I_{1}g|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\ dz,\end{array}

and
\displaystyle d
is defined in
Eq.

2.1
.

Subsequently, applying the Riesz potential bounds from
Lemma

3.7
, we have the following important corollary where we obtain an elliptic type condition in the space variable and the parabolic condition is required only on the time variable of the datum:

Corollary 2.5
.

Let
\displaystyle\frac{2n}{n+2}<p\leq n
and
\displaystyle u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega))
be a weak solution of
Eq.

1.1
under the assumption
Eq.

3.1
. And for any
\displaystyle\delta\in(0,1)
, let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{\bf a}
be defined in
Eq.

5.1
and
\displaystyle g\in L^{p^{\prime}}(0,T;L^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}(\Omega))
. Then there exists
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(n,p,\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1},\delta)
such that for any
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, there holds that

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{r}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p+\varepsilon}\ dz\apprle_{(n,p,\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1},\delta)}\lambda_{0}^{\varepsilon}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2r}(z_{0})}(|\nabla u|+1)^{p}\ dz+\fint_{I_{2r}(t_{0})}\left(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}(2r)^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}|g|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}\ dx\right)^{\frac{p}{(p-1)\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{(p-1)\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}}\ dt,

where
\displaystyle d
is from
Eq.

2.1
and

\lambda_{0}^{\frac{p}{d}}:=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2r}(z_{0})}(|\nabla u|+1)^{p}\ dz+\fint_{I_{2r}(t_{0})}\left(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}(2r)^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}|g|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}\ dx\right)^{\frac{p}{(p-1)\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}}\ dt.

In particular, if
\displaystyle|g(x,t)|\leq|f(x)|
, then we have

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{r}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p+\varepsilon}\ dz\apprle_{(n,p,\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1},\delta)}\lambda_{0}^{\varepsilon}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2r}(z_{0})}(|\nabla u|+1)^{p}\ dz+\left(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}(2r)^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}|f|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}\ dx\right)^{\frac{p}{(p-1)\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{(p-1)\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}}.

Remark 2.6
.

In
Corollary

2.5
, we note that the relation between
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}
and
\displaystyle\delta
is of the form
\displaystyle\varepsilon<\frac{p(1-\delta)}{p\delta-1}
. So in particular, as
\displaystyle\delta\rightarrow 0
, we automatically have
\displaystyle\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.

We now state the gradient potential estimate in the case of data independent of time.

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
\displaystyle u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega))
be a weak solution of
Eq.

8.1
and suppose that
\displaystyle\mathbf{F}
defined in
(
C8
)
-
(
C10
)
of
Definition

8.1
is finite. Furthermore let
\displaystyle z_{0}
be a Lebesgue point of
\displaystyle\nabla u
and assume
\displaystyle\mathbf{F}
is finite at
\displaystyle z_{0}
. Then there holds

|\nabla u(z_{0})|\apprle_{n,p}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p}+1\ dz\right)^{\frac{d}{p}}+\mathbf{F}^{\max\{1,\frac{1}{p-1}\}}(z_{0}),

where
\displaystyle d
is defined in
Eq.

2.1
.

3
Notations and Definitions

3.1
Structure of the operator and notion of solution

1.

For the results of
Section

5
, we assume
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(x,t,\nabla u)
is a Carathéodory function, i.e., we have
\displaystyle(x,t)\mapsto\mathcal{A}(x,t,\zeta)
is measurable for every
\displaystyle\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

\displaystyle\zeta\mapsto\mathcal{A}(x,t,\zeta)
is continuous for almost every
\displaystyle(x,t)\in\Omega_{T}
.

We further assume that for a.e.
\displaystyle(x,t)\in\Omega_{T}
and for any
\displaystyle\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there exist two positive constants
\displaystyle\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1}
, such that the following bounds are satisfied by the nonlinear structure:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}(x,t,\zeta)\ ,\zeta\rangle\geq\Lambda_{0}|\zeta|^{p}\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\mathcal{A}(x,t,\zeta)|\leq\Lambda_{1}|\zeta|^{p-1}.

(3.1)

2.

For the gradient potential estimate proved in
Section

8
, we take
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(x,t,\nabla u)=|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u
.

Definition 3.1
.

We say that
\displaystyle u\in C_{\loc}^{0}(I;L^{2}(B))\cap L^{p}(I;W^{1,p}(B))
is a weak solution of
Eq.

1.1
if

\iint_{Q}g\phi\ dz=-\iint_{Q}u\varphi_{t}\,dt+\iint_{Q}\langle\mathcal{A}(x,t,u,\nabla u)\ ,\nabla\varphi\rangle\,dz,

for all
\displaystyle\varphi\in C^{1}(\bar{Q})
which vanish on the parabolic boundary (see
(
N10
)
) of
\displaystyle Q
.

3.2
Orlicz Space

Let us recall some well known properties of Orlicz function spaces, see
[
1
, Chapter 8]
for the details.

Definition 3.2

(
\displaystyle N
-function)
.

We say that a function
\displaystyle\Phi:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is an
\displaystyle N
-function if it is a convex function satisfying

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{\Phi(s)}{s}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{\Phi(s)}{s}=\infty.

Definition 3.3

(
\displaystyle\Delta_{2}
condition)
.

An
\displaystyle N
-function
\displaystyle\Phi
is said to be
\displaystyle\Delta_{2}
-regular if there exists a constant
\displaystyle L>0
such that
\displaystyle\Phi(2s)\leq L\Phi(s)
for all
\displaystyle s\geq s_{0}
with a given
\displaystyle s_{0}
large.

Subsequently, we can define the Orlicz function space as follows:

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\displaystyle\Phi
be an
\displaystyle N
-function satisfying
\displaystyle\Delta_{2}
-regular condition, then the following function space is well defined and is a Banach space:

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{\Phi}(\Omega):=\left\{v:\int_{\Omega}\Phi(|v|)\ dx<\infty\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lVert v\rVert_{L^{\Phi}(\Omega)}:=\inf_{s>0}\left\{\int_{\Omega}\Phi\left(\frac{|v|}{s}\right)\ dx\leq 1\right\}.

If two
\displaystyle N
-functions
\displaystyle\Phi_{1}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
satify
\displaystyle 0<\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{\Phi_{1}(s)}{\Phi_{2}(s)}<\infty
, then
\displaystyle L^{\Phi_{1}}(\Omega)=L^{\Phi_{2}}(\Omega)
with norm equivalence.
For a given
\displaystyle N
-function, we define the conjugate
\displaystyle N
-function of
\displaystyle\Phi
, denoted by
\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}
, as

\tilde{\Phi}(s)=\max_{t\geq 0}(st-\Phi(t)).

Subsequently, the following generalized Hölder inequality holds (see
[
1
, Chapter 8]
for the details):

Lemma 3.5
.

For any
\displaystyle v\in L^{\Phi}(\Omega)
and
\displaystyle w\in L^{\tilde{\Phi}}(\Omega)
, we have

\left|\int_{\Omega}vw\ dx\right|\leq 2\lVert v\rVert_{L^{\Phi}(\Omega)}\lVert w\rVert_{L^{\tilde{\Phi}}(\Omega)}.

Let us take
\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s):=s\log(1+s)
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s):=(1+s)\log(1+s)-s
, then it is easy to see that

\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{\Phi_{1}(s)}{\Phi_{2}(s)}=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{\Phi}_{2}=\exp(s)-s-1.

Applying
[
12
, Lemma 8.6]
, we have

\lVert h\rVert_{L^{\Phi_{1}}(\Omega)}\approx\int_{\Omega}|h|\log\left(e+\frac{|h|}{\lVert h\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}}\right)\ dx.

(3.2)

Moreover, we trivialy have

\int_{\Omega}|h|\ dx\leq\int_{\Omega}|h|\log\left(e+\frac{|h|}{\lVert h\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}}\right)\ dx.

(3.3)

3.3
Riesz potential

In this subsection, let us recall some well known properties of Riesz potential defined as follows:

Definition 3.6
.

In
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the Riesz kernel denoted by
\displaystyle I_{\alpha}
for some
\displaystyle 0<\alpha<n
, is defined by

\displaystyle I_{\alpha}(x):=|x|^{\alpha-n}
.
The Riesz potential of any measurable function
\displaystyle h
is defined as follows:

I_{\alpha}h:=I_{\alpha}*h:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{h(y)}{|x-y|^{n-\alpha}}\ dy.

The following result on the boundedness of Riesz potential can be found in
[
19
, Lemma 1.34]
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\displaystyle 1\leq q<n
and
\displaystyle\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a domain with finite measure. Then for
\displaystyle q\leq s<q^{*}:=\frac{nq}{n-q}
,
\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}:=1-\left(\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{s}\right)
and
\displaystyle\gamma:=\frac{(1-n)r}{n}+1
, there holds

\lVert I_{1}h\rVert_{L^{s}(\Omega)}\leq C^{\frac{1}{r}}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{q}(\Omega)},

where
\displaystyle C=\frac{w_{n}}{\gamma}\left(\frac{|\Omega|}{w_{n}}\right)^{\gamma}
and
\displaystyle w_{n}
is a volume of unit ball in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The main result regarding Riesz potentials that will be used in this paper is the following representation for writing any measurable function in divergence form.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\displaystyle\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with finite measure and
\displaystyle h\in L^{s}(\Omega)
. We extend
\displaystyle h
to be zero outside
\displaystyle\Omega
, then there exists a vector field
\displaystyle\vec{h}\in L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\displaystyle h=\dv\vec{h}
satisfying the point-wise estimate

|\vec{h}(x)|\apprle_{(n)}I_{1}h(x).

Proof.

First we extend
\displaystyle h
to be zero outside
\displaystyle\Omega
and consider the Green’s function
\displaystyle G(x,y)
associated to
\displaystyle-\Delta
on the ball in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Now consider at the following problem:

-\Delta\phi=h\qquad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}.

From standard theory, we can then explicitly write the solution as
\displaystyle\phi=G*h=G*(-\Delta\phi)
from which it is easy to see that the following holds:

\Delta\phi(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Delta_{x}G(x,y)h(y)\ dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Delta_{x}G(x,y)(-\Delta\phi(y))\ dy.

Therefore, we can write
\displaystyle h(x)=-\dv_{x}(\nabla G*h)=:-\dv_{x}\vec{h}
, where
\displaystyle\nabla G*h
is applied component wise. Since the green function
\displaystyle G(x,y)
we consider was on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we know that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}G(x,y)|\apprle_{(n)}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n-1}}
from which we obtain the desired pointwise estimate.
∎

4
Some well known lemmas

In this section, let us recall some well known lemmas, the first of which is the well known Sobolev-Poincare inequality (for example, see
[
8
, Chapter I]
for the details).

Lemma 4.1
.

For some
\displaystyle\rho>0
, let
\displaystyle B_{\rho}(x_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For constants
\displaystyle\sigma,q,r\in[1,\infty)
and
\displaystyle\vartheta\in(0,1)
such that
\displaystyle-\frac{n}{\sigma}\leq\vartheta(1-\frac{n}{q})-(1-\vartheta)\frac{n}{r}
is satisfied, then for any
\displaystyle h\in W^{1,q}(B_{\rho}(x_{0}))
there holds

\fint_{B_{\rho}(x_{0})}\frac{\left|h\right|^{\sigma}}{\rho^{\sigma}}\ dx\apprle_{(n,q)}\left(\fint_{B_{\rho}(x_{0})}\frac{\left|h\right|^{q}}{\rho^{q}}+|\nabla h|^{q}\ dx\right)^{\frac{\vartheta\sigma}{q}}\left(\fint_{B_{\rho}(x_{0})}\frac{\left|h\right|^{r}}{\rho^{r}}\ dx\right)^{\frac{(1-\vartheta)\sigma}{r}}.

The next result we need the following standard iteration lemma (for example, see
[
11
, Lemma 6.1]
or
[
16
]
for the details):

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\displaystyle 0<r<R<\infty
be given and
\displaystyle h:[r,R]\to\mathbb{R}
be a non-negative and bounded function. Furthermore, let
\displaystyle\theta\in(0,1)
and
\displaystyle A,B,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\geq 0
be fixed constants and
suppose that

h(\rho_{1})\leq\theta h(\rho_{2})+\frac{A}{(\rho_{2}-\rho_{1})^{\gamma_{1}}}+\frac{B}{(\rho_{2}-\rho_{1})^{\gamma_{2}}},

holds for all
\displaystyle r\leq\rho_{1}<\rho_{2}\leq R
, then the following conclusion holds:

h(r)\apprle_{(\theta,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})}\frac{A}{(R-r)^{\gamma_{1}}}+\frac{B}{(R-r)^{\gamma_{2}}}.

Since we are studying structures with
\displaystyle p
-growth, we also need the following algebraic identities (see
[
11
, Chapter 8]
for the details).

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\displaystyle p\in(1,\infty)
, then for any two vectors
\displaystyle A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
(both non zero), the following holds:

\left||A|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}A-|B|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}B\right|^{2}\apprle_{(n,p)}\left(|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|A-B|^{2}\apprle_{(n,p)}\langle|A|^{p-2}A-|B|^{p-2}B\ ,A-B\rangle.

Moreover, in the case when
\displaystyle p\geq 2
, we have

|A-B|^{p}\apprle_{(n,p)}\left(|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|A-B|^{2},

and in the case when
\displaystyle p\leq 2
, there holds that

|A-B|^{p}\apprle_{(n,p)}\left||A|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}A-|B|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}B\right|^{2}+\left||A|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}A-|B|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}B\right|^{p}|A|^{\frac{(2-p)p}{2}}.

The following lemma is an elementary extension theorem and can be found in
[
19
, Theorem 1.63]
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\displaystyle 1\leq p<\infty
and
\displaystyle h\in W^{1,p}(B_{r}(x_{0}))
. Then there exists
\displaystyle\tilde{h}\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\displaystyle\tilde{h}=h
on
\displaystyle B_{r}(x_{0})
,
\displaystyle\spt\tilde{h}\subset B_{\frac{3}{2}r}(x_{0})
and

\int_{B_{\frac{3}{2}r}(x_{0})}|\nabla\tilde{h}|^{p}\ dx\apprle_{(n,p)}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|\nabla h|^{p}+r^{-p}|h|^{p}\ dx.

Let us now recall the well known Sobolev embedding result which can be found in
[
19
, Lemma 1.64]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\displaystyle B_{r}
be a ball with radius
\displaystyle r
and let
\displaystyle 1\leq p\leq n
and
\displaystyle 1\leq q\leq\frac{np}{n-p}
with
\displaystyle 1\leq q<\infty
, then we have

\left(\fint_{B_{r}}\left|\frac{h-\left(h\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{r}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{r}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{r}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{r}$}}}}{r}\right|^{q}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C(n,p)\left(\fint_{B_{r}}|\nabla h|^{p}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Here, we have taken
\displaystyle q\in(1,\infty)
is any number in the case
\displaystyle p=n
.

We will use the following Morrey embedding and can be found in
[
19
, Theorem 1.62]
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\displaystyle h\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
with
\displaystyle p>n
. Then
\displaystyle h\in C^{0,1-\frac{n}{p}}(\overline{\Omega})
and

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{x,y\in\overline{\Omega}}|h(x)-h(y)|\apprle_{n,p}|x-y|^{1-\frac{n}{p}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sup_{\Omega}|h|\apprle_{(n,p)}|\Omega|^{\frac{1}{n}}\left(\fint_{\Omega}|\nabla h|^{p}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

In the borderline case
\displaystyle p=n
, we have the following result, see
[
19
, Theorem 1.66]
for the details.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
\displaystyle h\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
where
\displaystyle\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is convex. Suppose there is a constant
\displaystyle M
such that

\int_{\Omega\cap B_{r}(x_{0})}|\nabla h|\ dx\leq Mr^{n-1},

for all
\displaystyle B_{r}(x_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then there exist positive constants
\displaystyle\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{0}(n)
and
\displaystyle C=C(n)
such that

\int_{\Omega}\exp\left(\frac{\sigma}{M}|h-(h)_{\Omega}|\right)\ dx\leq C(\diam\Omega)^{n},

where
\displaystyle\sigma<\frac{\sigma_{0}|\Omega|}{(\diam\Omega)^{n}}
.

We define the truncated function in space variable as follows:

Definition 4.8
.

Let
\displaystyle\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{r}(x_{0}))
be any cut-off function, then we denote

\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t):=\frac{\int_{B_{r}}\eta^{p}(x)u(x,t)\ dx}{\int_{B_{r}}\eta^{p}(x)\ dx}.

Note that we will keep track of the domain of the definition of the cut-off function
\displaystyle\eta
.

The proof of the following lemma can be found in
[
21
, Lemma 5.3]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\displaystyle B_{r}(x_{0})\subset\Omega
and
\displaystyle u(\cdot,t)\in L^{p}_{\loc}(\Omega)
for some
\displaystyle p>1
be given. Furthermore, let
\displaystyle\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{r}(x_{0}))
be a cut-off function such that the following is satisfied for some
\displaystyle\overline{c}>0
:

\sup_{x\in B_{r}(x_{0})}\eta(x)\leq\overline{c}\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\eta(x)\ dx,

then the following conclusion holds:

\int_{B_{r}}|u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{r}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{r}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{r}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{r}$}}}(t)|^{p}\ dx\apprle_{(n,p,\overline{c})}\int_{B_{r}}|u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t)|^{p}\ dx\apprle_{(n,p,\overline{c})}\int_{B_{r}}|u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{r}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{r}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{r}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{r}$}}}(t)|^{p}\ dx.

4.1
Notation

We shall clarify all the notation that will be used in this paper.

(N1)

We shall fix a point
\displaystyle z_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\Omega\times(0,T)=\Omega_{T}
.

(N2)

We shall use
\displaystyle\nabla
to denote derivatives with respect to the space variable
\displaystyle x
.

(N3)

We shall sometimes alternate between using
\displaystyle\dot{h}
,
\displaystyle\partial_{t}h
and
\displaystyle h^{\prime}
to denote the time derivative of a function
\displaystyle h
.

(N4)

We shall use
\displaystyle D
to denote the derivative with respect to both the space variable
\displaystyle x
and time variable
\displaystyle t
in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

(N5)

In what follows, we shall always assume the following bound holds:

\frac{2n}{n+2}<p<\infty.

(N6)

Let
\displaystyle z_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a point,
\displaystyle\rho,s>0
be two given parameters and let
\displaystyle\lambda\in[1,\infty)
. We shall use the following notations:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&I_{s}(t_{0}):=(t_{0}-s^{2},t_{0}+s^{2})\subset\mathbb{R},&\quad Q_{\rho,s}(z_{0}):=B_{\rho}(x_{0})\times I_{s}(t_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\\
&I_{s}^{\lambda}(t_{0}):=(t_{0}-\lambda^{2-p}s^{2},t_{0}+\lambda^{2-p}s^{2})\subset\mathbb{R},&\quad Q^{\lambda}_{\rho,s}(z_{0}):=B_{\rho}(x_{0})\times I^{\lambda}_{s}(t_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\\
&Q_{\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0}):=Q_{\rho,\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0}).\end{array}

(N7)

We shall use
\displaystyle\int
to denote the integral with respect to either space variable or time variable and use
\displaystyle\iint
to denote the integral with respect to both space and time variables simultaneously.

Analogously, we will use
\displaystyle\fint
and
\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint
to denote the average integrals as defined below: for any set
\displaystyle A\times B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
, we define

\displaystyle\displaystyle\left(h\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle A$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle A$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle A$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle A$}}}:=\fint_{A}h(x)\ dx=\frac{1}{|A|}\int_{A}h(x)\ dx,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\left(h\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle A\times B$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle A\times B$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle A\times B$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle A\times B$}}}:=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{A\times B}h(x,t)\ dx\ dt=\frac{1}{|A\times B|}\iint_{A\times B}h(x,t)\ dx\ dt.

(N8)

Given any positive function
\displaystyle\mu
, we shall denote
\displaystyle\left(h\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mu$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\mu$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mu$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}}}:=\int h\frac{\mu}{\|\mu\|_{L^{1}}}dm
where the domain of integration is the domain of definition of
\displaystyle\mu
and
\displaystyle dm
denotes the associated measure.

Moreover, we shall denote
\displaystyle\left(h\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mu$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\mu$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mu$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}}}^{r}:=\int_{B_{r}}h\frac{\mu}{\|\mu\|_{L^{1}}}dm
, where the superscript
\displaystyle r
is used to keep track of the domain of integration.

(N9)

We will use the notation
\displaystyle\apprle_{(a,b,\ldots)}
to denote an inequality with a constant depending on
\displaystyle a,b,\ldots
.

(N10)

For a given space-time cylinder
\displaystyle Q=B_{R}\times(a,b)
, we denote the parabolic boundary of
\displaystyle Q
to be the union of the bottom and the lateral boundaries, i.e.,
\displaystyle\partial_{p}Q=B_{r}\times\{t=a\}\bigcup\partial B_{R}\times(a,b)
.

(N11)

The exponent
\displaystyle\mathfrak{\bf a}
denotes a fixed value that will be given explicitly at the beginning of each section.

(N12)

We will denote
\displaystyle p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
to be the conjugate exponent,
\displaystyle p^{*}=\frac{np}{n-p}
to be the elliptic Sobolev exponent and
\displaystyle p^{\#}=\frac{p(n+2)}{n}
to be the prabolic Sobolev exponent.

5
Proof of
Theorem

2.2
- Borderline higher integrability.

The proof proceeds in several steps, essentially following the strategy developed in
[
13
]
. Before we begin the proof, let us fix some constants that will be needed later.

Definition 5.1
.

Let us define the Sobolev exponent as follows:

\mathfrak{\bf a}:=\begin{cases}(\delta p)^{*}:=\frac{n\delta p}{n-\delta p}&\text{ if }p\leq n,\\
\infty&\text{ if }p>n.\end{cases}

(5.1)

We also need the following choice of an exponent that will be needed when proving a reverse Hölder type inequality:

Definition 5.2
.

For some
\displaystyle\delta\in(0,1)
, we take

q:=\max\left\{\delta p,\frac{np}{n+2},\frac{2n}{n+2}\right\},

satisfying the following restrictions:

\delta\geq\frac{n}{n+2}\max\left\{1,\frac{2}{p}\right\},\qquad\delta>\frac{1}{p}\ \ \text{in the case}\ p\leq n\qquad\text{and}\qquad\delta>\frac{n}{p}\ \ \text{in the case}\ p>n.

(5.2)

Note that since
\displaystyle p>\frac{2n}{n+2}
, the above restrictions imply we can take
\displaystyle\delta<1
.

With these choices, we shall now define the required intrinsic setting:

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\displaystyle Q_{8\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})\subset\Omega_{T}
for some
\displaystyle\lambda\geq 1
. Furthermore, let us assume the following intrinsic bounds are satisfied throughout this section:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}\left(|\nabla u|+1\right)^{p}\ dz+\lambda^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}\left(1-\frac{p}{\mathfrak{\bf a}}\right)}\fint_{B_{\rho}(x_{0})}\rho^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}|f|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}\ dz\geq\lambda^{p}.,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{4\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}\left(|\nabla u|+1\right)^{p}\ dz+\lambda^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}\left(1-\frac{p}{\mathfrak{\bf a}}\right)}\fint_{B_{4\rho}(x_{0})}(4\rho)^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}|f|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}\ dz\leq\lambda^{p},.

(5.4)

5.1
Caccioppoli type estimate

Suppose that
\displaystyle B_{4\rho}(x_{0})\subset\Omega
and
\displaystyle\rho\leq\rho_{a}<\rho_{b}\leq 4\rho
. Consider the following cut-off function:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\eta=\eta(x)\in C_{c}^{\infty}\left(B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})\right),&\eta\equiv 1\ \,\text{on}\ B_{\rho_{a}}(x_{0}),&0\leq\eta\leq 1,&|\nabla\eta|\leq\frac{c}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}.\end{array}

(5.5)

Then from the restriction
\displaystyle\rho\leq\rho_{a}<\rho_{b}\leq 4\rho
, it is clear that

\sup_{x\in B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\eta(x)\apprle_{(n)}\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\eta(x)\ dx.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\displaystyle u
be a weak solution of
Eq.

1.1
and
\displaystyle B_{4\rho}(x_{0})\subset\Omega
. Then for
\displaystyle\rho\leq\rho_{a}<\rho_{b}\leq 4\rho
and
\displaystyle\eta
defined in
Eq.

5.5
, the following holds:

\begin{array}[]{l}\lambda^{p-2}\sup_{I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}}\right|^{2}\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\ dx+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p}\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\ dz\\
\hskip 85.35826pt\apprle_{(n,p,\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right|^{p}\ dz+\lambda^{p-2}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right|^{2}\eta^{p}\zeta\ dz\\
\hskip 85.35826pt\qquad\qquad+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|f||u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)|\ dz.\end{array}

Proof.

Consider the following cut-off functions:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\zeta=\zeta(t)\in C_{c}^{\infty}\left(I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0})\right),&\zeta\equiv 1\ \,\text{on}\ I_{\rho_{a}}^{\lambda}(t_{0}),&0\leq\zeta\leq 1,&|\partial_{t}\zeta|\leq\frac{c}{\lambda^{2-p}(\rho_{b}-\rho_{a})^{2}}.\end{array}

Let us make use of
\displaystyle\left(u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)\right)\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}
as a test function in
Eq.

1.1
. Then the proof of Caccioppoli inequality follows verbatim as in
[
21
, Lemma 5.4]
. There is one crucial cancellation that was used in
[
21
, Lemma 5.4]
and to highlight its importance, we recall that here. Using the test function in the time derivative, we see that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\iint_{Q_{\rho_{b}}}u\frac{d}{dt}\left[\left(u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)\right)\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\right]&=&\iint_{Q_{\rho_{b}}}\left(u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)\right)\frac{d}{dt}\left[\left(u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)\right)\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\right]\\
&&+\iint_{Q_{\rho_{b}}}\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)\frac{d}{dt}\left[\left(u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)\right)\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\right],\end{array}

which requires us to estimate the last term which would not exist if
\displaystyle\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}(t)
was independent of time. But the crucial cancellation that we need provides that this term is zero, which can be formally seen as follows:

\int_{I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\zeta^{2}(t)\underbrace{\left(\int_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}u(x,t)\eta^{p}(x)\ dx-\frac{\int_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\eta^{p}(x)u(x,t)\ dx}{\int_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\eta^{p}(x)\ dx}\int_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\eta^{p}(x)\ dx\right)}_{=0}\frac{d\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{dt}\ dt=0.

Once we have this cancellation, the rest of the Caccioppoli estimate follows verbatim as in
[
13
,
22
,
23
]
.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

We will use the notation
\displaystyle\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)
with the superscript
\displaystyle\rho_{b}
to denote that the cut-off function
\displaystyle\eta
is supported on
\displaystyle B_{\rho_{b}}
since we need to keep track of this radius in several forthcoming estimates.

5.2
Some intermediate estimates

We need the following important estimate that will make use of Sobolev embedding:

Lemma 5.6
.

Recall the choice of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{\bf a}
from
Eq.

5.1
and suppose
Eq.

5.4
holds. Then for any
\displaystyle\rho\leq r\leq 4\rho
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\fint_{I_{r}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\left(\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t)}{r}\right|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{\bf a}}}dt\apprle\lambda\qquad\text{and}\qquad\fint_{I_{r}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\left(\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t)}{r}\right|^{2}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dt\apprle\lambda.

Note that when
\displaystyle\mathfrak{\bf a}=\infty
, we use the notation
\displaystyle\left(\fint|f|^{\infty}\right)^{\frac{1}{\infty}}:=\|f\|_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Let us prove the first estimate. We shall split the proof into two cases depending on the choice of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{\bf a}
as given in
Eq.

5.1
.

Case
\displaystyle p\leq n
:

In this case, we can apply Sobolev Poincare inequality in the space variable to get

\fint_{I_{r}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\left(\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t)}{r}\right|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{\bf a}}}\ dt\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sob_poin}}}{\apprle}\left(\fint_{I_{r}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\left(\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|\nabla u|^{\delta p}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta p}}\ dt\right)\overset{\lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{le_la}and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{def_de}}{\apprle}\lambda.

Case
\displaystyle p>n
:

In this case, note that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}=1
. Let us first apply
Lemma

4.6
to extend
\displaystyle u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t)
by a function
\displaystyle v
on
\displaystyle B_{2r}(x_{0})
. Thus we get the following sequence of estimates:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sup_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t)\right|\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ext}}}{\leq}\sup_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|v|&\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{Morrey}}}{\apprle}&r\left(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|\nabla v|^{p}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ext}}}{\apprle}&r\left(\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p}+\frac{\left|u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t)\right|^{p}}{r^{p}}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\overset{\hypertarget{pna}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}}}{\apprle}&r\left(\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\end{array}

where to obtain
(
a
)
, we applied Poincare’s inequality. Thus, we get

\fint_{I_{r}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\sup_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{\left|u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t)\right|}{r}\ dt\apprle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{r}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p}\ dz\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\overset{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{le_la}}{\apprle}\lambda.

(5.6)

For the second estimate, we note that since
\displaystyle p>\frac{2n}{n+2}
, we have the Sobolev exponent
\displaystyle p^{*}=\frac{np}{n-p}>2
and thus, we can apply the standard Sobolev Poincare inequality to get the required estimate.
∎

We now prove an upper bound for the time term from
Lemma

5.4
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Assuming
Lemma

5.4
holds, then for any
\displaystyle 2\rho\leq r\leq 4\rho
, we have

\sup_{I_{r}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t)}{r}\right|^{2}\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\ dx\apprle\lambda^{2},

where
\displaystyle\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{r}(x_{0})),\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(I_{r}^{\lambda})(t_{0})
with
\displaystyle\eta\equiv 1
on
\displaystyle B_{s}(x_{0})
and
\displaystyle\zeta\equiv 1
on
\displaystyle I_{s}^{\lambda}(t_{0})
for some
\displaystyle\rho\leq s\leq r-\frac{\rho}{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle\rho\leq\rho_{a}\leq\frac{3}{2}\rho
and
\displaystyle 2\rho\leq\rho_{b}\leq 4\rho
by any two radii, which implies
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\rho\leq\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}\leq 3\rho
. In the notation of the lemma, this is the choice
\displaystyle s=\rho_{a}
and
\displaystyle r=\rho_{b}
and
\displaystyle\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0}))
with
\displaystyle|\nabla\eta|\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}
and
\displaystyle\zeta\in C^{\infty}(I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0}))
with
\displaystyle|\partial_{t}\zeta|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2-p}(\rho_{b}-\rho_{a})^{2}}
. Let us make use of
Lemma

5.4
and estimate each of the terms on the right-hand side of
Lemma

5.4
to get:

Estimate for the first term:

In order to estimate this, we proceed as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right|^{p}\ dz\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sob_poin}}}{\apprle}\left(\frac{\rho_{b}}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right)^{p}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p}\ dz\overset{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{le_la}}{\apprle}\left(\frac{\rho_{b}}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right)^{p}\lambda^{p}\apprle\lambda^{p},\end{array}

where to obtain the last estimate, we made use of the restrictions on
\displaystyle\rho_{a}
and
\displaystyle\rho_{b}
.

Estimate for the second term:

We proceed as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}II&=&\lambda^{p-2}\fint_{I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right|\eta^{\frac{p}{2}}\zeta\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right|\eta^{\frac{p}{2}}\zeta\ dx\ dt\\
&\leq&\lambda^{p-2}\fint_{I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\left(\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right|^{2}\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right|^{2}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ dt\\
&\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sobolev_bound}}}{\apprle}&\lambda^{p-1}\left(\sup_{I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right|^{2}\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{array}

Therefore applying Young’s inequality, for any
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rcl}II&\apprle&\varepsilon\lambda^{p-2}\sup_{I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}-\rho_{a}}\right|^{2}\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\ dx+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lambda^{p}.\end{array}

Estimate for the third term:

This is an important term to estimate and we proceed as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&&\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|f||u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)|\ dz\\
&&\leq\fint_{I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\left(\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}(\rho_{b})^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}|f|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}}\left(\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}}\right|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{\bf a}}}\ dt\\
&&\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sobolev_bound}}}{\apprle}\left(\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}(\rho_{b})^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}|f|^{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{\bf a}^{\prime}}}\lambda\overset{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{le_la}}{\leq}\lambda^{p}.\end{array}

Thus combining the above estimates into
Lemma

5.4
and recalling the restrictions on
\displaystyle\rho\leq\rho_{a}\leq 2\rho
and
\displaystyle 3\rho<\rho_{b}\leq 4\rho
, we get

\lambda^{p-2}\sup_{I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}}\right|^{2}\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\ dx\apprle\varepsilon\lambda^{p-2}\sup_{I_{\rho_{b}}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{\rho_{b}}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{\rho_{b}}(t)}{\rho_{b}}\right|^{2}\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\ dx+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lambda^{p}.

Choosing
\displaystyle\varepsilon
small enough, the proof of the lemma follows.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

With notation as in
Lemma

5.7
, there holds

\sup_{I_{s}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\frac{1}{|B_{r}(x_{0})|}\int_{B_{s}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{s}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{s}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{s}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{s}(x_{0})$}}}(t)}{r}\right|^{2}\ dx\apprle\lambda^{2}.

Proof.

From triangle inequality, we have that
\displaystyle\int_{A}|f-\left(f\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle A$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle A$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle A$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle A$}}}|^{q}\ dx\leq 2^{q-1}\inf_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{A}|f-z|^{q}\ dx
where
\displaystyle A
is some measurable set, we obtain the following sequence of inequalities:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sup_{I_{s}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\frac{1}{|B_{r}(x_{0})|}\int_{B_{s}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{s}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{s}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{s}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{s}(x_{0})$}}}(t)}{r}\right|^{2}\ dx&\apprle&\sup_{I_{s}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\frac{1}{|B_{r}(x_{0})|}\int_{B_{s}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{r}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{r}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{r}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{r}(x_{0})$}}}(t)}{r}\right|^{2}\ dx\\
&\apprle&\sup_{I_{r}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{r}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{r}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{r}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{r}(x_{0})$}}}(t)}{r}\right|^{2}\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\ dx\\
&\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{weight_lem}}}{\apprle}&\sup_{I_{r}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\eta$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\eta$}}}^{r}(t)}{r}\right|^{2}\eta^{p}\zeta^{2}\ dx\\
&\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sup_2_bnd}}}{\apprle}&\lambda^{2}.\end{array}

∎

5.3
Reverse Hölder inequality in an intrinsic cylinder

Lemma 5.9
.

Assuming
Lemma

5.4
holds and with notation as in
Lemma

5.7
, for any
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the following holds:

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}}(t)}{2\rho}\right|^{p}\ dz\apprle_{(n,p,\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1},\delta)}\varepsilon\lambda^{p}+C(\varepsilon)\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{q}\ dz\right)^{\frac{p}{q}}.

Recall that
\displaystyle q
is from
Definition

5.2
.

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

4.1
with
\displaystyle\sigma=p
,
\displaystyle q
as defined in
Definition

5.2
,
\displaystyle r=2
and
\displaystyle\vartheta=\frac{q}{p}
. It is easy to see that the choice of exponents satisfies the hypothesis of
Lemma

4.1
thanks to the restriction from
Definition

5.2
. In particular, we have

-\frac{n}{2}\leq\vartheta(1-\frac{n}{q})-(1-\vartheta)\frac{n}{2}\quad\Longleftarrow\quad\frac{np}{n+2}\leq q.

Therefore, we have the following sequence of estimates:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}}(t)}{2\rho}\right|^{p}\ dz&\overset{\hypertarget{6.17a}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}}}{\leq}&\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{q}\ dz\right)\left(\sup_{I_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{2\rho}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{2\rho}(x_{0})$}}}(t)}{2\rho}\right|^{2}\ dx\right)^{\frac{p(1-\vartheta)}{2}}\\
&\overset{\hypertarget{6.17c}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}}}{\leq}&\lambda^{p(1-\vartheta)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{q}\ dz,\end{array}

where to obtain
(
a
)
, we made use of
Lemma

4.1
followed by Poincaré inequality and to obtain
(
b
)
, we made use of
Corollary

5.8
applied with
\displaystyle s=2\rho
and
\displaystyle r=3\rho
. Finally, an application of Young’s inequality gives the desired result.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Assuming
Lemma

5.4
holds and with notation as in
Lemma

5.7
, for any
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the following holds:

\lambda^{p-2}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{2\rho}$}}}(t)}{2\rho}\right|^{2}\ dz\apprle_{(n,p,\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1},\delta)}\varepsilon\lambda^{p}+C(\varepsilon)\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.86662pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-14.04051pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.90251pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-9.15251pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{q}\ dz\right)^{\frac{p}{q}}.

Recall that
\displaystyle q
is from
Definition

5.2
.

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

4.1
with
\displaystyle\sigma=2
,
\displaystyle q
as defined in
Definition

5.2
,
\displaystyle r=2
and any
\displaystyle\vartheta\in(0,1)
is admissible since

-\frac{n}{2}\leq\vartheta(1-\frac{n}{q})-(1-\vartheta)\frac{n}{2}\quad\Longleftarrow\quad\frac{2n}{n+2}\leq q.

Let us take
\displaystyle\vartheta\in(0,1)
such that
\displaystyle 0<\vartheta<\frac{q}{2}
, then we get

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&&\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{2\rho}$}}}(t)}{2\rho}\right|^{2}\ dz\\
&&\apprle\fint_{I_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\left(\fint_{B_{2\rho}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{2\rho}$}}}(t)}{2\rho}\right|^{q}+|\nabla u|^{q}\ dx\right)^{\frac{2\vartheta}{q}}\left(\fint_{B_{2\rho}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{2\rho}$}}}(t)}{2\rho}\right|^{2}\ dx\right)^{1-\vartheta}\ dt\\
&&\overset{\hypertarget{6.23a}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}}}{\leq}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{q}\ dz\right)^{\frac{2\vartheta}{q}}\left(\sup_{I_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(t_{0})}\fint_{B_{2\rho}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{u-\left(u\right)_{\mathchoice{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textstyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle B_{2\rho}$}}{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle B_{2\rho}$}}}(t)}{2\rho}\right|^{2}\ dx\right)^{1-\vartheta}\\
&&\overset{\hypertarget{6.23b}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}}}{\apprle}\lambda^{2(1-\vartheta)}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-15.55559pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-13.52937pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-11.41374pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin]{18.0}{$\displaystyle-\mkern-9.5mu-$}$}}\kern-10.66374pt}}\!\iint_{Q_{2\rho}^{\lambda}(z_{0})}|\nabla u|^{q}\ dz\right)^{\frac{2\vartheta}{q}}.\end{array}
